\end{center}

% =====================================================================
% PAPER [42]
% =====================================================================

\newpage
\begin{center}
\textbf{[42]}\\[4pt]
\textbf{\large ON THE CAUSTIC BY REFLECTION AT A CIRCLE.}\\[4pt]
\textit{[From the Cambridge and Dublin Mathematical Journal, vol.~ii. (1847), pp.~128---130.]}
\end{center}

\vspace{6pt}

The following solution of the problem is that given by M.~de~St-Laurent (Annales de Gergonne, t.~xvii. [1826] pp.~128---134); the process of elimination is somewhat different.

The centre of the circle being taken for the origin, let $k$~be its radius; $a$,~$b$ the coordinates of the luminous point; $\xi$,~$\eta$ those of the point at which the reflection takes place; $x$,~$y$ those of any point in the reflected ray: we have in the first place
\[
\xi^2 + \eta^2 = k^2 \qquad(1).
\]

There is no difficulty in finding the equation of the reflected ray\footnote{To do this in the simplest way, write
\[
\rho^2 = (\xi-x)^2 + (\eta-y)^2, \quad \sigma^2 = (\xi-a)^2 + (\eta-b)^2,
\]
then, by the condition of reflection,
\[
\rho + \sigma = \text{min.},
\]
$\xi$,~$\eta$ being considered as functions of the variables $\xi$,~$\eta$ which are connected by the equation~(1). Hence
\[
\frac{\xi-x}{\rho} + \frac{\xi-a}{\sigma} = \lambda\xi, \quad \frac{\eta-y}{\rho} + \frac{\eta-b}{\sigma} = \lambda\eta,
\]
or, eliminating~$\lambda$,
\[
\eta\left(\frac{\xi-x}{\rho} + \frac{\xi-a}{\sigma}\right) - \xi\left(\frac{\eta-y}{\rho} + \frac{\eta-b}{\sigma}\right) = 0,
\]
whence $(\eta x - \xi y)\frac{1}{\rho} = [(\xi-a)^2 + (\eta-b)^2] = (\eta a - \xi b)^2 \, [(\xi-x)^2 + (\eta-y)^2]$.

This may be written
\begin{multline*}
\{(\eta x - \xi y)(\xi-a) - (\eta a - \xi b)(\xi-x)\} \, \{ (\eta x - \xi y)(\xi-a) + (\eta a - \xi b)(\xi-x) \} \\
+ \{ (\eta x - \xi y)(\eta-b) - (\eta a - \xi b)(\eta-y) \} \, \{ (\eta x - \xi y)(\eta-b) + (\eta a - \xi b)(\eta-y) \} = 0;
\end{multline*}
the factors in $\{\}$ reduce themselves respectively to $\xi P$ and $\eta P$, where $P = \eta(x-y) - \xi(a-x) + a\eta - bx$; omitting the factor~$P$, (which equated to zero, is the equation of the line through $(a,b)$ and $(\xi,\eta)$,) and replacing $\xi(\xi-a) + \eta(\eta-b)$ and $\xi(\xi-x) + \eta(\eta-y)$ by $k^2 - a\xi - b\eta$ and $k^2 - \xi x - \eta y$, respectively, we have the equation given above. C.~35}; this is
\[
(\eta x - a\eta)(\xi x + \eta y - k^2) + (\eta y - x\eta)(a\xi + b\eta - k^2) = 0 \qquad(2),
\]

\newpage
or, arranging the terms in a more convenient order,
\[
(bx + ay)(\xi^2 - \eta^2) + 2(by - ax)\,\xi\eta - k^2(b+y)\xi + k^2(a+x)\eta = 0 \qquad(2').
\]

Hence, considering $\xi$,~$\eta$ as indeterminate parameters connected by the equation~(1), the locus of the curve generated by the continued intersections of the lines~(2) will be found by eliminating $\xi$,~$\eta$,~$\lambda$ from these equations and the system
\begin{align*}
\xi[\lambda + 2(bx+ay)] + \eta[2(by-ax)] - k^2(b+y) &= 0 \qquad(3),\\
\xi[2(by-ax)] + \eta[\lambda - 2(bx+ay)] + k^2(a+x) &= 0 \qquad(4),
\end{align*}
and from these, multiplying by $\xi$,~$\eta$, adding and reducing by~(2), we have
\[
-\xi^2(b+y) + \eta^2(a+x) - k^2\lambda = 0 \qquad(5),
\]
which replaces the equation~(2) or~$(2')$. Thus the equations from which $\xi$,~$\eta$,~$\lambda$ are to be eliminated are (1),~(3),~(4),~(5).

From (3), (4), (5), by the elimination of $\xi$,~$\eta$, we have
\begin{multline*}
-\lambda\{k^4 - 4(bx+ay)^2\} - k^2(by-ax)(a+x)(b+y) \\
- k^2(a+x)^2[\lambda + 2(bx+ay)] \\
- k^2(b+y)^2[\lambda - 2(bx+ay)] \\
+ k^2\lambda(by-ax)^2 = 0 \qquad(6),
\end{multline*}
or, reducing,
\[
-\lambda^2 + \lambda\,\{4(a^2+b^2)(x^2+y^2) - k^2[(a+x)^2 + (b+y)^2]\} - 3k^2(bx-ay)(x^2+y^2-a^2-b^2) = 0 \qquad(7);
\]
which may be represented by
\[
-\lambda^3 + \lambda\,Q - 2R = 0 \qquad(7').
\]

Again, from the equations (4), (3), transposing the last terms and adding the squares, also reducing by~(1),
\begin{multline*}
k^4\,[(a+x)^2 + (b+y)^2] = k^4\lambda^2 + 4k^4(a^2+b^2)(x^2+y^2) \\
+ k^2\lambda\{(y^2-x^2)(bx+ay) + 2xy(by-ax)\} \qquad(8);
\end{multline*}
but from the same equations, multiplying by $\xi$,~$\eta$ and adding, also reducing by~(1),
\[
k^2\lambda + 2(bx+ay)(\xi^2-\eta^2) + 4\xi\eta(by-ax) + k^2[-\xi(b+y) + \eta(a+x)] = 0 \qquad(9),
\]
or reducing by~(5) and dividing by two,
\[
k^2\lambda + (bx+ay)(\xi^2-\eta^2) + 2\xi\eta(by-ax) = 0 \qquad(10).
\]

\newpage
Using this to reduce~(8),
\[
\tfrac{1}{2}k^2\,[(a+x)^2 + (b+y)^2] = \tfrac{1}{2}(a^2+b^2)(x^2+y^2) + 3\lambda^2 \qquad(11),
\]
or, from the value of~$P$,
\[
-3\lambda^2 + Q = 0 \qquad(12),
\]
which singularly enough is the derived equation of~$(7')$ with respect to~$\lambda$: so that the equation of the curve is obtained by expressing that two of the roots of the equation~$(7')$ are equal. Multiplying (12) by~$\lambda$ and reducing by~$(7')$,
\[
-\lambda Q + 3R = 0,
\]
or, combining this with~(12),
\[
\lambda : Q : R = 3 : 6\lambda : \lambda Q = 3 : 6\lambda : 2Q;
\]
whence, replacing $R$,~$Q$ by their values, we find
\begin{multline*}
27k^6(bx-ay)^2(x^2+y^2-a^2-b^2) \\
- \{4(a^2+b^2)(x^2+y^2) - k^2[(a+x)^2+(b+y)^2]\}^3 = 0,
\end{multline*}
the equation of M.~de~St-Laurent.

\begin{center}
C.~35
\end{center}

\vspace{20pt}
\begin{center}
\rule{0.5\textwidth}{0.4pt}
\end{center}

% =====================================================================
% PAPER [43]
% =====================================================================

\newpage
\begin{center}
\textbf{[43]}\\[4pt]
\textbf{\large ON THE DIFFERENTIAL EQUATIONS WHICH OCCUR IN\\DYNAMICAL PROBLEMS.}\\[4pt]
\textit{[From the Cambridge and Dublin Mathematical Journal, vol.~ii. (1847), pp.~210---219.]}
\end{center}

\vspace{6pt}

Jacobi, in a very elaborate memoir, ``Theoria novi multiplicatoris systemati aequationum differentialium vulgarium applicandi''\footnote{Crelle, t.~xxvii. [1844], pp.~199---268 and t.~xxix. [1845], pp.~213---279 and 333---376. Compare also the memoir in Liouville, t.~x. [1845], pp.~337---346.}, has demonstrated a remarkable property of an extensive class of differential equations, namely, that when all the integrals of the system except a single one are known, the remaining integral can always be determined by a quadrature. Included in the class in question are, as Jacobi proceeds to show, the differential equations corresponding to any dynamical problem in which neither the forces nor the equations of condition involve the velocities; i.e.~in all ordinary dynamical problems, when all the integrals but one are known, the remaining integral can be determined by quadratures. In the case where the forces and equations of condition are likewise independent of the time, it is immediately seen that the system may be transformed into a system in which the number of equations is less by unity than in the original one, and which does not involve the time, which may afterwards be determined by a quadrature\footnote{For, representing the velocities by $x'$,~$y'$\ldots\ the dynamical system takes the form
\[
dt : dx : dy\ldots : dx' : dy'\ldots = 1 : x' : y'\ldots : X : Y\ldots,
\]
and the system in question is simply $dx : dy\ldots : dx' : dy'\ldots = x' : y'\ldots : X : Y\ldots$.}; and, Jacobi's theorem applying to this new system, he arrives at the proposition ``In any dynamical problem where the forces and equations of condition contain only the coordinates of the different points of the system, when all the integrals but two are determined, the remaining integrals may be found by quadratures only.'' In the following paper, which contains the demonstrations of these propositions, the analysis employed by Jacobi has been considerably varied in the details, but the leading features of it are preserved.

\vspace{10pt}

\papersection{1} Let the variables $x$,~$y$,~$z$,\ldots\ \&c.\ be connected with the variables $u$,~$v$,~$w$,\ldots\ by the same number of equations, so that the variables of each set may be considered as functions of those of the other set. And assume
\[
dx\,dy\ldots = V\,du\,dv\ldots;
\]
if from the functions which equated to zero express the relations between the two sets of variables we form two determinants, the former with the differential coefficients of these functions with respect to $u$,~$v$,\ldots\ and the latter with the differential coefficients of the same functions with respect to $x$,~$y$,\ldots\ the quotient with its sign changed obtained by dividing the first of these determinants by the second is, as is well known, the value of the function~$V$.

Putting for shortness
\[
\frac{dx}{du} = \alpha, \quad \frac{dy}{du} = \beta, \quad \&\text{c.} \qquad
\frac{dx}{dv} = \alpha', \quad \frac{dy}{dv} = \beta', \quad \&\text{c.}
\]
\[
\frac{du}{dx} = A, \quad \frac{du}{dy} = B, \quad \&\text{c.} \qquad
\frac{dv}{dx} = A', \quad \frac{dv}{dy} = B', \quad \&\text{c.}
\]
$V$~is the reciprocal of the determinant formed with $A$,~$B$,\ldots; $A'$,~$B'$,\ldots, \&c.; or it is the determinant formed with $\alpha$,~$\beta$,\ldots\ $\alpha'$,~$\beta'$,\ldots, \&c.

From the first of these forms, i.e.\ considering $V$ as a function of $A$,~$B$,\ldots
\[
\frac{dV}{dA} = \frac{\alpha}{V}, \quad \frac{dV}{dB} = \frac{\beta}{V}, \quad \&\text{c.} \qquad
\frac{dV}{dA'} = \frac{\alpha'}{V}, \quad \frac{dV}{dB'} = \frac{\beta'}{V}, \quad \&\text{c.},
\]
where the quantities $\alpha$,~$\beta$,\ldots\ $\alpha'$,~$\beta'$,\ldots\ and $A$,~$B$,\ldots\ $A'$,~$B'$,\ldots\ may be interchanged provided $-V$ be substituted for~$V$. (Demonstrations of these formul\ae\ or of some equivalent to them will be found in Jacobi's memoir ``De determinantibus functionalibus,'' Crelle, t.~xxii. [(1841) pp.~319---359].)

Hence
\[
\frac{1}{V}\,dV + \alpha\,dA + \beta\,dB + \ldots - \alpha'\,dA' + \beta'\,dB' + \ldots = 0,
\]
or reducing by
\[
\frac{dA}{dy} = \frac{dB}{dx}, \quad \frac{dA'}{dy} = \frac{dB'}{dx}, \quad \&\text{c.},
\]
this becomes
\begin{multline*}
\frac{1}{V}\,dV + \alpha\left(\frac{dA}{dx}\,dx + \frac{dB}{dx}\,dy + \ldots\right) + \beta\left(\frac{dA}{dy}\,dx + \frac{dB}{dy}\,dy + \ldots\right) + \ldots \\
- \alpha'\left(\frac{dA'}{dx}\,dx + \frac{dB'}{dx}\,dy + \ldots\right) + \beta'\left(\frac{dA'}{dy}\,dx + \frac{dB'}{dy}\,dy + \ldots\right) + \ldots = 0;
\end{multline*}

\newpage
or, reducing,
\begin{multline*}
\frac{1}{V}\,dV + \left(\frac{dA}{dx} + \frac{dA'}{dx} + \ldots\right)(\alpha\,dx + \beta\,dy + \ldots) \\
+ \left(\frac{dB}{dx} + \frac{dB'}{dx} + \ldots\right)(\alpha'\,dx + \beta'\,dy + \ldots) + \ldots = 0;
\end{multline*}
whence separating the differentials and replacing $A$,~$A'$,\ldots; $B$,~$B'$,\ldots; by their values
\begin{align*}
\frac{1}{V}\,\frac{dV}{dx} &= \frac{d}{du}\,\frac{du}{dx} + \frac{d}{dv}\,\frac{dv}{dx} + \ldots,\\
\frac{1}{V}\,\frac{dV}{dy} &= \frac{d}{du}\,\frac{du}{dy} + \frac{d}{dv}\,\frac{dv}{dy} + \ldots,
\end{align*}
(in which $-V$, $u$,~$v$\ldots; $x$,~$y$\ldots\ may be substituted for $V$, $x$,~$y$\ldots; $u$,~$v$\ldots).

\vspace{10pt}

\papersection{2} Let $X$,~$Y$\ldots\ be any functions of the variables $x$,~$y$,\ldots\ and assume
\[
U = \frac{du}{dx}\,X + \frac{du}{dy}\,Y + \ldots, \qquad
V = \frac{dv}{dx}\,X + \frac{dv}{dy}\,Y + \ldots,
\]
$U$,~$V$,\ldots\ being expressed in terms of $u$,~$v$\ldots\ Then
\begin{align*}
\frac{dU}{du} + \frac{dV}{dv} + \ldots &= \Sigma\left(\frac{d}{du}\,\frac{du}{dx} + \frac{d}{dv}\,\frac{dv}{dx} + \ldots\right)X + \Sigma\left(\frac{d}{du}\,\frac{du}{dy} + \frac{d}{dv}\,\frac{dv}{dy} + \ldots\right)Y + \ldots \\
&= \Sigma\left(\frac{dX}{du}\,\frac{du}{dx} + \frac{dX}{dv}\,\frac{dv}{dx} + \ldots\right) + \Sigma\left(\frac{dY}{du}\,\frac{du}{dy} + \frac{dY}{dv}\,\frac{dv}{dy} + \ldots\right) + \ldots \\
&= \Sigma\,\frac{dU}{dx} + \Sigma\,\frac{dV}{dy} + \ldots = \Sigma\left(\frac{dV}{dx} + \frac{dV}{dy} + \ldots\right),
\end{align*}
i.e.
\[
\frac{dU}{du} + \frac{dV}{dv} + \ldots = \frac{dX}{dx} + \frac{dY}{dy} + \ldots
\]
Also, whatever be the value of~$M$,
\[
\frac{d}{du}\,MV\,U + \frac{d}{dv}\,MV\,V + \ldots = \frac{d}{dx}\,MV\,X + \frac{d}{dy}\,MV\,Y + \ldots,
\]
and from these two properties,
\[
\frac{d\,MV\,U}{du} + \frac{d\,MV\,V}{dv} + \ldots = \nabla\left(\frac{d\,M\,X}{dx} + \frac{d\,M\,Y}{dy} + \ldots\right).
\]

\vspace{10pt}

\papersection{3} Consider the system of differential equations
\[
dx : dy : dz\ldots = X : Y : Z\ldots
\]
(where, for greater clearness, an additional letter~$z$ has been introduced). From these we deduce the equivalent system
\[
du : dv : dw\ldots = U : V : W\ldots.
\]

\newpage
Suppose that $u$ and~$v$ continue to represent arbitrary functions of $x$,~$y$,~$z$,\ldots\ but that the remaining functions $w$,\ldots\ are such as to satisfy $W=0$,\ldots\ (so that $w$,\ldots\ may be considered as the constants introduced by obtaining all the integrals but one of the system of differential equations in $x$,~$y$,~$z$,\ldots), we have
\[
\frac{d\,MV\,U}{du} + \frac{d\,MV\,V}{dv} = \nabla\left(\frac{d\,M\,X}{dx} + \frac{d\,M\,Y}{dy} + \frac{d\,M\,Z}{dz} + \ldots\right).
\]
Also the only one of the transformed equations which remains to be integrated is
\[
du : dv = U : V, \quad \text{or}\quad V\,du - U\,dv = 0,
\]
(in which it is supposed that $U$ and~$V$ are expressed by means of the other integrals in terms of $u$ and~$v$).

Suppose $M$~can be so determined that
\[
\frac{d\,M\,X}{dx} + \frac{d\,M\,Y}{dy} + \frac{d\,M\,Z}{dz} + \ldots = 0,
\]
($M$~is what Jacobi terms the multiplier of the proposed system of differential equations): then
\[
\frac{d\,MV\,U}{du} + \frac{d\,MV\,V}{dv} = 0,
\]
or $MV$~is the multiplier of $V\,du - U\,dv = 0$, so that
\[
\int MV\,(V\,du - U\,dv) = \text{const.}
\]

Hence the theorem:---``Given a multiplier of the system of equations
\[
dx : dy : dz,\ldots = X : Y : Z\ldots
\]
(the meaning of the term being defined as above), then if all the integrals but one of this system are known, the remaining integral depends upon a quadrature.''

Jacobi proceeds to discuss a variety of different systems of equations in which it is possible to determine the multiplier~$M$. Among the most important of these may be considered the system corresponding to the general problem of Dynamics, which may be discussed under three different forms.

\vspace{10pt}

\papersection{4} Lagrange's first form\footnote{I have slightly modified the form so as to avoid the introduction of the masses, and to allow $x$ (for instance) to stand for any one of the coordinates of any of the points, instead of standing for a coordinate parallel to a particular axis.}.

Let the \textit{whole series} of coordinates, each of them multiplied by the square root of the corresponding mass, be represented by $x$,~$y$,\ldots\ and in the same way the whole series of forces, each of them multiplied by the square root of the corresponding mass, by $P$,~$Q$,\ldots; then the equations of motion are
\[
\frac{d^2x}{dt^2} = X, \quad \frac{d^2y}{dt^2} = Y, \ldots;
\]
where
\[
X = P + \lambda\,\frac{d\Theta}{dx} + \mu\,\frac{d\Phi}{dx} + \ldots,
\]
where $\Theta = 0$, $\Phi = 0$,\ldots\ are the equations of condition connecting the variables, and $\lambda$,~$\mu$,\ldots\ coefficients to be determined by substituting the values of $\frac{d^2x}{dt^2}$, \&c.\ in the equations $\Theta = 0$, $\frac{d\Theta}{dt} = 0$, \&c.\ It is supposed that as well $P$,~$Q$,\ldots\ as $\Theta$,~$\Phi$,\ldots\ are independent of the velocities.

\newpage
In order to reduce these to an analogous form to that previously employed, we have only to write
\[
\frac{dx}{dt} = x', \quad \frac{dy}{dt} = y', \ldots;
\]
which gives
\begin{multline*}
dt : dx : dy : dz\ldots : dx' : dy' : dz'\ldots \\
= 1 : x' : y' : z'\ldots : X : Y : Z\ldots
\end{multline*}
Supposing that $M$~is independent of $x'$,~$y'$,~$z'$,\ldots\ the equation on which it depends becomes immediately
\[
\delta M + M\left(\frac{dX}{dx'} + \frac{dY}{dy'} + \frac{dZ}{dz'} + \ldots\right) = 0,
\]
where for shortness
\[
\delta = \frac{d}{dt} + x'\frac{d}{dx} + y'\frac{d}{dy} + z'\frac{d}{dz} + \ldots
\]
To reduce this we must first determine the values of $\lambda$,~$\mu$\ldots, and for this we have
\[
\frac{d^2\Theta}{dt^2} = \frac{d\Theta}{dx}\,\frac{d^2x}{dt^2} + \frac{d\Theta}{dy}\,\frac{d^2y}{dt^2} + \ldots + x'^2\frac{d^2\Theta}{dx^2} + \ldots = 0,
\]
i.e.
\[
\frac{d\Theta}{dx}\,X + \frac{d\Theta}{dy}\,Y + \ldots + x'^2\frac{d^2\Theta}{dx^2} + \ldots = 0,
\]

\newpage
where for greater clearness an additional letter of the series $\Theta$,~$\Phi$\ldots\ has been introduced, and where
\[
h = \Sigma\left(\frac{d\Theta}{dx}\right)^2, \quad
a = -\frac{d\Theta}{dx}\,\frac{d\Phi}{dx} - \frac{d\Theta}{dy}\,\frac{d\Phi}{dy} - \ldots
\]
Hence differentiating with respect to~$x'$,
\begin{align*}
\frac{d\Theta}{dx}\,\frac{dX}{dx'} + \frac{d\Theta}{dy}\,\frac{dY}{dx'} + \ldots + \frac{d\lambda}{dx'}\,h + \frac{d\mu}{dx'}\,a + \ldots &= 0,\\
\frac{d\Phi}{dx}\,\frac{dX}{dx'} + \frac{d\Phi}{dy}\,\frac{dY}{dx'} + \ldots + \frac{d\lambda}{dx'}\,a + \frac{d\mu}{dx'}\,b + \ldots &= 0,\\
\&\text{c.};
\end{align*}
or representing by~$K$ the determinant formed with the quantities $a$,~$h$,~$g$,\ldots\ $h$,~$b$,~$f$,\ldots\ $g$,~$f$,~$c$,\ldots\ and by $A$,~$H$,~$G$,\ldots\ $H$,~$B$,~$F$,\ldots\ $G$,~$F$,~$C$,\ldots\ the inverse system of coefficients, we have
\begin{align*}
\frac{dX}{dx'} &= A\,\frac{d\Theta}{dx} + H\,\frac{d\Phi}{dx} + \ldots,\\
\frac{dY}{dy'} &= H\,\frac{d\Theta}{dy} + B\,\frac{d\Phi}{dy} + \ldots,\\
2(G\,\frac{d\Theta}{dz} + F\,\frac{d\Phi}{dz} + C\,\frac{d\Psi}{dz}\ldots) + K\frac{dZ}{dz'} &= 0,
\end{align*}
whence, multiplying by $\frac{dx'}{dx}$, $\frac{dy'}{dy}$, $\frac{dz'}{dz}$,\ldots\ and adding,
\[
A\delta a + B\delta b + C\delta c\ldots + 2F\delta f + 2G\delta g + 2H\delta h\ldots + K\left(\frac{dX}{dx'} + \frac{dY}{dy'} + \frac{dZ}{dz'} + \ldots\right) = 0;
\]
and, forming the similar equations with the remaining variables and adding,
\[
A\delta a + B\delta b + C\delta c\ldots + 2F\delta f + 2G\delta g + 2H\delta h\ldots + K\left(\frac{dX}{dx'} + \frac{dY}{dy'} + \frac{dZ}{dz'}\ldots\right) = 0:
\]
i.e.
\[
\delta K + K\left(\frac{dX}{dx'} + \frac{dY}{dy'} + \frac{dZ}{dz'} + \ldots\right) = 0,
\]
or
\[
\frac{dX}{dx'} + \frac{dY}{dy'} + \frac{dZ}{dz'} + \ldots = -\frac{\delta K}{K}.
\]

\begin{center}
C.~36
\end{center}

\newpage
Thus the equation in~$M$ reduces itself to
\[
\delta M - M\frac{\delta K}{K} = 0,
\]
which is satisfied by $M = K$. It may be remarked that $K$~reduces itself to the sum of the squares of the different functional determinants formed with the differential coefficients of $\Theta$,~$\Phi$\ldots\ with respect to the different combinations of as many variables out of the series $x$,~$y$\ldots

\vspace{10pt}

\papersection{5} Lagrange's second form.

Here the equations of motion are assumed to be
\begin{align*}
\frac{d}{dt}\,\frac{dT}{dx'} - \frac{dT}{dx} - P &= 0,\\
\frac{d}{dt}\,\frac{dT}{dy'} - \frac{dT}{dy} - Q &= 0,\\
\frac{d}{dt}\,\frac{dT}{dz'} - \frac{dT}{dz} - R &= 0,
\end{align*}
where $2T$~represents the \textit{vis viva} of the system, $x$,~$y$,~$z$,\ldots\ are the independent variables on which the solution of the problem depends, and $x'$,~$y'$,~$z'$,\ldots\ their differential coefficients with respect to the time. It is assumed as before $P$,~$Q$,~$R$\ldots\ do not contain $x'$,~$y'$,~$z'$,\ldots.

Suppose these equations give
\begin{multline*}
dt : dx : dy : dz\ldots : dx' : dy' : dz'\ldots \\
= 1 : x' : y' : z'\ldots : X : Y : Z\ldots;
\end{multline*}
then the equation which determines the multiplier~$M$ takes as before the form
\[
\delta M + M\left(\frac{dX}{dx'} + \frac{dY}{dy'} + \frac{dZ}{dz'} + \ldots\right) = 0.
\]
To reduce this equation, substituting for~$T$ its value which is of the form
\[
T = \tfrac{1}{2}(ax'^2 + by'^2 + cz'^2\ldots + 2fy'z' + 2gz'x' + 2hx'y'\ldots),
\]
and putting for shortness
\begin{align*}
L &= ax' + hy' + gz'\ldots,\\
M &= hx' + by' + fz'\ldots,\\
N &= gx' + fy' + cz'\ldots
\end{align*}

\newpage
the equations which determine $X$,~$Y$,~$Z$\ldots\ are
\begin{align*}
aX + hY + gZ\ldots + \delta L - P &= 0,\\
hX + bY + fZ\ldots + \delta M - Q &= 0,\\
gX + fY + cZ\ldots + \delta N - R &= 0.
\end{align*}
Hence, differentiating with respect to~$x'$,
\begin{align*}
a\,\frac{dX}{dx'} + h\,\frac{dY}{dx'} + g\,\frac{dZ}{dx'}\ldots + \frac{d\,\delta L}{dx'} - \frac{dP}{dx'} &= 0,\\
h\,\frac{dX}{dx'} + b\,\frac{dY}{dx'} + f\,\frac{dZ}{dx'}\ldots + \frac{d\,\delta M}{dx'} - \frac{dQ}{dx'} &= 0,\\
g\,\frac{dX}{dx'} + f\,\frac{dY}{dx'} + c\,\frac{dZ}{dx'}\ldots + \frac{d\,\delta N}{dx'} - \frac{dR}{dx'} &= 0,
\end{align*}
or representing by~$K$ the determinant formed with $a$,~$h$,~$g$,\ldots\ $h$,~$b$,~$f$,\ldots\ $g$,~$f$,~$c$,\ldots\ and by $A$,~$H$,~$G$,\ldots\ $H$,~$B$,~$F$,\ldots\ $G$,~$F$,~$C$\ldots\ the inverse system of coefficients, we have
\begin{align*}
\frac{dL}{dz} &= a\,\frac{dX}{dx'} + h\,\frac{dY}{dx'} + g\,\frac{dZ}{dx'}\ldots + \frac{d\,\delta L}{dx'} = 0,\\
\frac{dM}{dz} &= h\,\frac{dX}{dx'} + b\,\frac{dY}{dx'} + f\,\frac{dZ}{dx'}\ldots + \frac{d\,\delta M}{dx'} = 0,\\
\frac{dN}{dz} &= g\,\frac{dX}{dx'} + f\,\frac{dY}{dx'} + c\,\frac{dZ}{dx'}\ldots + \frac{d\,\delta N}{dx'} = 0.
\end{align*}
and similarly
\begin{align*}
Ka + \delta h + B\delta b + F\delta f\ldots + H\left(\frac{dM}{dy} - \frac{dL}{dx}\right) + F\left(\frac{dN}{dy} - \frac{dM}{dz}\right)\ldots &= 0,\\
Kg + \delta h + B\delta b + F\delta f\ldots + H\left(\frac{dM}{dy} - \frac{dL}{dx}\right) + *\left(\frac{dN}{dy} - \frac{dM}{dz}\right)\ldots &= 0,\\
K\,\frac{dZ}{dz'} + f\left(\frac{dL}{dy} - \frac{dM}{dx}\right) + \ldots + h\left(\frac{dL}{dz} - \frac{dN}{dx}\right)\ldots &= 0.
\end{align*}
Hence, adding,
\[
K\left(\frac{dX}{dx'} + \frac{dY}{dy'} + \frac{dZ}{dz'}\ldots\right) + A\delta a + B\delta b + C\delta c\ldots + 2F\delta f + 2G\delta g + 2H\delta h\ldots = 0;
\]
and thus we have as before, though with symbols bearing an entirely different signification,
\[
\delta M + M\left(\frac{dX}{dx'} + \frac{dY}{dy'} + \frac{dZ}{dz'} + \ldots\right) = 0,
\]
and thence $\delta M - M\frac{\delta K}{K} = 0$, and $M = K$.

\begin{center}
36---2
\end{center}

\newpage
(The value of~$K$ in this section may I think be conveniently termed ``the determinant of the \textit{vis viva},'' with respect to the variables $x$,~$y$,~$z$,\ldots---It may be remarked that ``the determinant of the \textit{vis viva}'' with respect to any other system of variables $u$,~$v$,~$w$,\ldots\ is $=V^2 K$, $V$~as before.)

\vspace{10pt}

\papersection{6} Third form of the equations of motion. [Hamiltonian form.]

Here writing
\[
\xi = \frac{dT}{dx'}, \quad \eta = \frac{dT}{dy'}, \ldots;
\]
and taking $t$,~$x$,~$y$,\ldots\ $\xi$,~$\eta$,\ldots\ for the variables of the problem [and considering $T$ to be expressed as a function of these variables: to denote this change it would have been proper to use instead of~$T$ a new letter~$\mathfrak{T}$] the equations of motion reduce themselves to
\[
\frac{d\xi}{dt} = -\frac{dT}{dx} + P, \quad \frac{d\eta}{dt} = -\frac{dT}{dy} + Q, \ldots;
\]
or putting for shortness
\begin{align*}
\frac{d\xi}{dt} &= -\frac{dT}{dx} + X, &
\frac{dx}{dt} &= \frac{dT}{d\xi},\\
\frac{d\eta}{dt} &= -\frac{dT}{dy} + Y, &
\frac{dy}{dt} &= \frac{dT}{d\eta},\\
\frac{d\zeta}{dt} &= -\frac{dT}{dz} + Z, &
\frac{dz}{dt} &= \frac{dT}{d\zeta},\ldots
\end{align*}
they become
\begin{multline*}
dt : dx : dy : dz\ldots : d\xi : d\eta : d\zeta\ldots \\
= 1 : \frac{dT}{d\xi} : \frac{dT}{d\eta} : \frac{dT}{d\zeta}\ldots : X : Y : Z\ldots
\end{multline*}
Hence writing the equation in~$M$ under the form
\begin{multline*}
\delta M + M\left(\frac{d}{d\xi}\,\frac{dT}{dx} + \frac{d}{d\eta}\,\frac{dT}{dy} + \ldots + \frac{d}{dx}\,\frac{dT}{d\xi} + \frac{d}{dy}\,\frac{dT}{d\eta} + \ldots\right) \\
+ M\left(\frac{dX}{d\xi} + \frac{dY}{d\eta} + \frac{dZ}{d\zeta} + \ldots\right) = 0,
\end{multline*}
where $\delta = \frac{d}{dt} + \frac{dT}{d\xi}\,\frac{d}{dx} + \frac{dT}{d\eta}\,\frac{d}{dy} + \ldots + X\,\frac{d}{d\xi} + Y\,\frac{d}{d\eta} + \ldots$

we see immediately that ($P$,~$Q$\ldots\ being as before independent of the velocities, and consequently of $\xi$,~$\eta$,~$\zeta$,\ldots),
\[
\frac{dX}{d\xi} = \frac{d^2T}{dx\,d\xi} = 0, \quad \frac{dY}{d\eta} = \frac{d^2T}{dy\,d\eta} = 0, \quad \&\text{c.}
\]
Hence $\delta M = 0$, which is satisfied by $M = 1$.

\vspace{20pt}
\begin{center}
\rule{0.5\textwidth}{0.4pt}
\end{center}

% =====================================================================
% PAPER [44]
% =====================================================================

\newpage
\begin{center}
\textbf{[44]}\\[4pt]
\textbf{\large ON A MULTIPLE INTEGRAL CONNECTED WITH THE\\THEORY OF ATTRACTIONS.}\\[4pt]
\textit{[From the Cambridge and Dublin Mathematical Journal, vol.~ii. (1847), pp.~219---223.]}
\end{center}

\vspace{6pt}

Mr Boole [in the Memoir ``On a Certain Multiple definite Integral'' Irish Acad.\ Trans.\ vol.~XXI. (1848), pp.~40---150] has given for the integral with $n$~variables
\[
\int\!\!\int \frac{\phi(\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} + \ldots)\,dx\,dy\ldots}{(x^2 + y^2\ldots + u^2)^{\frac{1}{2}n+i} \, \{(x-\alpha)^2 + (y-\beta)^2\ldots + v^2\}^{\frac{1}{2}n+j}},
\]
limits $\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} + \ldots - 1$,
the following formula, or one which may readily be reduced to that form\footnote{See note at the end of this paper.},
\[
\frac{\pi^{\frac{1}{2}n}\,\Gamma i}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}n+i)} \int_0^\infty \frac{S\,ds}{(Rs+f)^{\frac{1}{2}n+i} \,(s+S^{-1})^{\frac{1}{2}n+j-1}} \qquad(1'),
\]
where
\[
S = \frac{a^{s-1}b^{s-1}\ldots}{\pi^{\frac{1}{2}n}} \int_0^1 dt \, t^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1-t)^{\frac{1}{2}n-1} \phi[\sigma + t(1-\sigma)] \qquad(3);
\]
in which
\[
\sigma = \frac{a^2}{a^2+\eta}, \quad \frac{b^2}{b^2+\eta}, \ldots \qquad(4)
\]
and $\eta$~is determined by
\[
\frac{\alpha^2}{a^2+\eta} + \frac{\beta^2}{b^2+\eta} + \ldots + \frac{v^2}{\eta} = 1. \qquad(5')
\]

\vspace{10pt}

Suppose $f = g = \ldots = \infty$; also assume
\[
\phi(\xi) = (f^2\xi + v^2)^{-\frac{1}{2}n-q'},
\]
then the integral becomes
\begin{multline*}
\int\!\!\int \frac{dx\,dy\ldots}{(x^2+y^2\ldots+u^2)^{\frac{1}{2}n+i} \, \{(x-\alpha)^2 + (y-\beta)^2\ldots + v^2\}^{\frac{1}{2}n+q}} \\
= \int\!\!\int \frac{dx\,dy\ldots}{(x^2+y^2\ldots+u^2)^{\frac{1}{2}n+i} \, \{(x-\alpha)^2 + (y-\beta)^2\ldots + v^2\}^{\frac{1}{2}n+q}} \qquad(6'),
\end{multline*}
the limits for each variable being $-\infty$,~$\infty$.

Now, writing $f^2s$ for~$s$ and $f^2\eta$ for~$\eta$, the new value of~$\eta$ reduces itself to zero, and
\[
\frac{\pi^{\frac{1}{2}n}\,\Gamma i}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}n+i)} \int_0^\infty \frac{S\,ds}{(Rs+f^2)^{\frac{1}{2}n+i}}.
\]
Also $\sigma = 0$; but
\[
S = \frac{a^{s-1}b^{s-1}\ldots}{\pi^{\frac{1}{2}n}} \int_0^1 dt \, t^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1-t)^{\frac{1}{2}n-1} (f^2t + v^2)^{-\frac{1}{2}n-q},
\]
where $r^2 = \alpha^2 + \beta^2 + \ldots$ whence also putting $\frac{v^2}{f^2}$ for~$t$, $\phi[\sigma + t(1-\sigma)]$ becomes
\[
(f^2 - \sigma + t(1-\sigma) + v^2)^{-\frac{1}{2}n-q} = \frac{1}{(t + \frac{v^2}{f^2})^{\frac{1}{2}n+q}},
\]
i.e.
\[
\xi = r^2 \cdot \frac{1}{1+s} + \frac{v^2}{s}.
\]
Hence
\[
\frac{\Gamma(-q)\,\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}n+q)}{\pi^{\frac{1}{2}n}\,\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}n+q+q')} \cdot \frac{p^{2q}}{(t + \frac{v^2}{f^2})^{\frac{1}{2}n+q}},
\]
or substituting in~$U$, and replacing $A$ by its value,
\[
\frac{\pi^{\frac{1}{2}n}\,\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}n+q+q')}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}n+i)\,\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}n+q')} \int_0^\infty \frac{s^{-\frac{1}{2}n-q-q'}\,ds}{\left(\frac{r^2}{1+s} + \frac{v^2}{s}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}n+i}},
\]
or, what comes to the same,
\[
\int_0^\infty \frac{s^{-\frac{1}{2}n-q-q'}\,ds}{\{s(r^2+v^2) + r^2\}^{\frac{1}{2}n+i}},
\]
where
\[
J = m^2 + n^2 + v^2.
\]

\newpage
The only practicable case is that of $q' = -q$, for which
\[
\frac{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}n+q)\,\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}n-q)}{\pi^{\frac{1}{2}n}\,\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}n+i)} \int_0^\infty \frac{s^{-\frac{1}{2}n}\,ds}{\{(s+\frac{1}{2})^2 + v^2\}^{\frac{1}{2}n+i}} \qquad(7').
\]
Consider the more general expression
\[
\Theta = \int_0^\infty s^{-\frac{1}{2}n-q} \left(\frac{s}{s+\frac{1}{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}n+q+i} \phi\left(\frac{u^2}{s} + \frac{v^2}{s+\frac{1}{2}}\right) ds \qquad(9);
\]
by writing $2uv\surd s = \surd(s^2 + 4uv) \pm \surd s'$,
the upper sign from $s = \infty$ to $s = \frac{1}{2}$, and the lower one from $s = \frac{1}{2}$ to $s = 0$, it is easy to derive
\[
\Theta = \frac{\pi^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}n+q)} \int_0^\infty \frac{e^{-u}\,du}{u^{\frac{1}{2}n+q}} \int_0^\infty s^{-\frac{1}{2}n-q} (s+4uv)^{-\frac{1}{2}n-q} e^{-\frac{u^2}{s}} ds \qquad(10).
\]
Now, by a formula which will presently be demonstrated,
\[
\int_0^\infty s^{-\frac{1}{2}n-q} (s+4uv)^{-\frac{1}{2}n-q} e^{-\frac{u^2}{s}} ds = \frac{\pi^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}n+q)} \cdot \frac{e^{-2uv}}{(4uv)^{\frac{1}{2}n+q-\frac{1}{2}}} \int_0^\infty s^{-\frac{1}{2}n-q} (s+4uv)^{-\frac{1}{2}n-q} e^{-s} ds \qquad(11);
\]
whence
\begin{multline*}
\int_0^\infty \frac{\{\surd(s+4uv) + \surd s\}^{-\frac{1}{2}n-q} + \{\surd(s+4uv) - \surd s\}^{-\frac{1}{2}n-q}}{\surd s\,\surd(s+4uv)}\,ds \\
= \frac{\pi^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}n+q)} \cdot \frac{e^{-2uv}}{(uv)^{\frac{1}{2}n+q-\frac{1}{2}}} \qquad(12).
\end{multline*}
Thus, by merely changing the function,
\[
\Theta = \frac{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}-q)}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}n+q)} \int_0^\infty (s+4uv)^{-\frac{1}{2}n-q} \left(\phi(s+\xi) - \frac{d\phi}{d\xi}\right) (uv)^{\frac{1}{2}n+q-1}\,ds \qquad(13);
\]
and hence in the particular case in question
\[
U = \frac{\pi^{\frac{1}{2}n}\,\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}n+q)}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}n+i)} \int_0^\infty s^{-\frac{1}{2}n-q} (s+4uv)^{-\frac{1}{2}n-q} (s+\xi+2uv)^{-\frac{1}{2}n+i}\,ds \qquad(14),
\]
by means of the formula
\[
\frac{d^{\frac{1}{2}n}}{ds^{\frac{1}{2}n}}\,e^{-s} = (-)^{\frac{1}{2}n}\,s^{-\frac{1}{2}n}\,e^{-s}.
\]
But as there may be some doubt about this formula, which is not exactly equivalent either to Liouville's or Peacock's expression for the general differential coefficient of a

\newpage
power, it is worth while to remark that, by first transforming the $\frac{1}{2}n$\textsuperscript{th} power into an exponential, and then reducing as above (thus avoiding the general differentiation), we should have obtained
\[
\frac{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}-q)\,\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}n+q)\,\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}n-q)}{\pi^{\frac{1}{2}n}\,\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}n+i)} \int_0^\infty \int_0^\infty \frac{e^{-s}\,ds\,d\theta}{(s+\xi+2uv\cos\theta)^{\frac{1}{2}n+i}},
\]
which reduces itself to the equation~(14) by simply performing the integration with respect to~$\theta$; thus establishing the formula beyond doubt\footnote{A paper by M.~Schl\"omilch ``Note sur la variation des constantes arbitraires d'une Int\'egrale d\'efinie,'' Crelle, t.~xxviii. [1846], pp.~268---280, will be found to contain formules analogous to some of the preceding ones.}. The integral may evidently be effected in finite terms when either $\frac{1}{2}n$ or $\frac{1}{2}n-q$~is integral. Thus for instance in the simplest case of all, or when $q = -\frac{1}{2}$,
\[
\int\!\!\int \frac{dx\,dy\ldots}{(x^2+y^2\ldots+u^2)^{\frac{1}{2}n+1} \, \{(x-\alpha)^2 + \ldots + v^2\}^{\frac{1}{2}n-\frac{1}{2}}} = \frac{\pi^{\frac{1}{2}n+1}}{(n+1)\,(v^2+2uv)^{\frac{1}{2}n}} \cdot \frac{1}{(u^2+v^2\ldots+v^2)^{\frac{1}{2}n-1} \, \{(x-\alpha)^2 + \ldots + v^2\}^{\frac{1}{2}n-1}},
\]
a formula of which several demonstrations have already been given in the Journal.

The following is a demonstration, though an indirect one, of the formula~(11): in the first place
\begin{multline*}
\int_0^\infty \frac{\{\surd(s+4uv) + \surd s\}^{-\frac{1}{2}n-q} + \{\surd(s+4uv) - \surd s\}^{-\frac{1}{2}n-q}}{\surd s\,\surd(s+4uv)}\,ds \\
= \frac{\pi^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}n+q)} \cdot \frac{e^{-2uv}}{(uv)^{\frac{1}{2}n+q-\frac{1}{2}}} \int_0^\infty \frac{e^{-u}\,du}{u^{\frac{1}{2}n+q}} \int_0^\infty s^{-\frac{1}{2}n-q} (s+4uv)^{-\frac{1}{2}n-q} e^{-\frac{u^2}{s}} ds \qquad(16).
\end{multline*}
(where as usual $i = \surd{-1}$): to prove this, we have
\[
\int_0^\infty (4u^2v^2 + x^2)^{\frac{1}{2}n-1} e^{ixs}\,dx = \frac{\pi^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}n+q)} \cdot \frac{e^{-2uvs}}{(2uv)^{\frac{1}{2}n+q-1}},
\]
\[
\int_0^\infty \frac{dx}{(4u^2v^2+x^2)^{\frac{1}{2}n+q}} = \frac{\pi^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}n+q)} \cdot \frac{1}{(2uv)^{2\frac{1}{2}n+2q-1}} \int_0^\infty dt \, t^{-\frac{1}{2}n-q} e^{-u^2t - \frac{4u^2v^2}{t}};
\]
\[
= \frac{\pi^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}n+q)\,(2uv)^{\frac{1}{2}n+q-1}} \int_0^\infty dt \, t^{-\frac{1}{2}n-q} e^{-u^2t - \frac{4u^2v^2}{t}},
\]
or, putting $iuv\,\surd t = \surd(s+4uv) + \surd s$ (which is a transformation already employed in the present paper), the formula required follows immediately.

Now, by a formula due to M.~Catalan, but first rigorously demonstrated by M.~Serret,
\[
\int_0^\infty (4u^2v^2 + x^2)^{\frac{1}{2}n-1} e^{ixs}\,dx = \frac{\pi^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}n+q)} \cdot \frac{e^{-2uvs}}{(2uv)^{\frac{1}{2}n+q-1}},
\]
(Liouville, t.~viii. [1843] p.~1), and by a slight modification in the form of this equation
\[
\int_0^\infty (4u^2v^2 + x^2)^{\frac{1}{2}n-1} e^{ixs}\,dx = \frac{\pi^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}n+q)} \cdot \frac{e^{-2uvs}}{(2uv)^{\frac{1}{2}n+q-1}},
\]
\[
\int_0^\infty \frac{e^{-s}\,ds}{s^{\frac{1}{2}n+q}\,(s+4uv)^{\frac{1}{2}n+q}} = \frac{\pi^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}n+q)} \cdot \frac{e^{-2uv}}{(uv)^{\frac{1}{2}n+q-\frac{1}{2}}},
\]
which, compared with (16), gives the required equation.

\vspace{10pt}
\textbf{Note.}---One of the intermediate formul\ae\ of Mr~Boole [in the Memoir referred to] may be written as follows: or what comes to the same thing, putting $t = \surd{-1}$, and rejecting the impossible part of the integral,
\[
S = -\int_0^\infty \int_0^\infty d\alpha \int_0^\pi dv\,\psi\,e^{i(a-\sigma)z} \cos[(a-\sigma)z + \tfrac{1}{2}q\pi]\,\phi\alpha,
\]
\[
S = \int_0^\infty \int_0^\pi d\alpha\,dz \int_0^\pi \frac{dv}{\pi} \psi\,e^{i(a-\sigma)z} \cos[(a-\sigma)z + \tfrac{1}{2}q\pi]\,\phi\alpha,
\]
Now $(a-\sigma)$ being positive,
\[
I = -\frac{e^{i\cdot\frac{1}{2}q\pi}}{\pi}\,\Gamma(q+1)\,e^{i(q+1)\pi}\,(a-\sigma)^{-q-1};
\]
or, retaining the real part only,
\[
I = \sin\,\tfrac{1}{2}q\pi\,\Gamma(q+1)\,(a-\sigma)^{-q-1};
\]
i.e.
\[
I = \frac{\sin\,\tfrac{1}{2}q\pi}{\pi}\,\Gamma(q+1)\,(a-\sigma)^{-q-1}.
\]
But $(a-\sigma)$ being negative,
\[
I = -\frac{e^{i\cdot\frac{1}{2}q\pi}}{\pi}\,\Gamma(q+1)\,e^{-i(q+1)\pi}\,(\sigma-a)^{-q-1};
\]
i.e.
\[
I = -\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{2}i q\pi}}{\pi}\,\Gamma(q+1)\,(\sigma-a)^{-q-1},
\]
or, retaining the real part only, $I = 0$.

Hence
\[
S = \frac{\sin\,\tfrac{1}{2}q\pi}{\pi}\,\Gamma(q+1) \int_0^\infty (a-\sigma)^{-q-1}\,\phi\alpha\,d\alpha,
\]
or putting $a = \sigma + t(1-\sigma)$, or $a-\sigma = t(1-\sigma)$,
\[
S = \frac{\sin\,\tfrac{1}{2}q\pi}{\pi}\,\Gamma(q+1) \int_0^1 t^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1-t)^{\frac{1}{2}n-1}\,\phi[\sigma + t(1-\sigma)]\,dt;
\]
the expression in the text. Mr~Boole's final value is
\[
S = \frac{\Gamma(q+1)}{\pi} \int_0^1 t^{q}(1-t)^{-q-1}\,\phi[\sigma + t(1-\sigma)]\,dt,
\]
which, though simpler, appears to me to be in some respects less convenient.

\begin{center}
C.~37
\end{center}

\vspace{20pt}
\begin{center}
\rule{0.5\textwidth}{0.4pt}
\end{center}

% =====================================================================
% PAPER [45]
% =====================================================================

\newpage
\begin{center}
\textbf{[45]}\\[4pt]
\textbf{\large ON THE THEORY OF ELLIPTIC FUNCTIONS.}\\[4pt]
\textit{[From the Cambridge and Dublin Mathematical Journal, vol.~ii. (1847), pp.~256---266.]}
\end{center}

\vspace{6pt}

Adopting the notation of the Fund.\ Nova, except that for shortness $\sno u$, $\cno u$, $\dno u$ are written instead of $\operatorname{sinam} u$, $\operatorname{cosam} u$, $\operatorname{\Delta am} u$, let the functions $\Theta(u)$, $\mathrm{H}(u)$ be defined by the equations
\[
\Theta u = C \, e^{\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{E-K}{K}\right)u^2} \int_0^{2K} \!\!\int_0^{2K} du\,du \, \dno^2 u \qquad(1),
\]
\[
\mathrm{H}\,u = -i\,e^{\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{E-K}{K}\right)(u+iK')^2} \, \Theta(u+iK') \qquad(2),
\]
it is required from these equations to express $\sno u$ in terms of the functions $\mathrm{H}(u)$, $\Theta(u)$. To accomplish this we have
\begin{align*}
\frac{d^2}{du^2} \log \sno u &= \frac{1}{\sno u}\,\frac{d^2}{du^2}\,\sno u - \frac{1}{\sno^2 u}\left(\frac{d}{du}\,\sno u\right)^2 \\
&= -(1+k^2) + 2k^2\,\sno^2 u - \{1 - (1+k^2)\,\sno^2 u + k^2\,\sno^4 u\} \\
&= k^2\,\sno^2 u - \frac{1}{\sno^2 u};
\end{align*}
whence also
\[
\frac{d^2}{du^2} \log \sno u = k^2\,\sno^2 u - k^2\,\sno^2(u+iK').
\]
If for a moment
\[
\psi_n\,u = \int_0^u du\,\sno^2 u,
\]
then
\[
\log \sno u = k^2\psi_1\,u - k^2\psi_1(u+iK') - \frac{Au}{2K} + B;
\]

\newpage
or writing $-u$ for $u$ and subtracting, $\psi_n u$ being an even function,
\[
2Au = \frac{i\pi u}{2K} - k^2\psi_n(iK'-u) + k^2\psi_n(iK'+u),
\]
or putting $u = K$,
\[
2AK = \frac{i\pi K}{2K} - k^2\psi_n(iK'-K) + k^2\psi_n(iK'+K).
\]
Now $\sno^2(u+K) - \sno^2(u-K) = 0$,
and therefore $\psi_n(u+K) - \psi_n(u-K) = 2\psi_n K$,
or $\psi_n(iK'+K) - \psi_n(iK'-K) = 2iK'\psi_n K$.

Also $E(u) = u - k^2\psi_n u$,
\[
E = K - k^2\psi_n K, \quad \text{i.e.}\quad \psi_n K = \frac{K-E}{k^2}.
\]
Hence
\[
\frac{i\pi}{2K} = k^2\psi_n u - k^2\psi_n(u+iK') + \frac{u}{2K}\{(K-E) - K'k^2\} + B',
\]
\[
\log \sno u = \log \Theta(u+iK') - \log \Theta u + \frac{i\pi u}{2K} + B';
\]
or, changing the constant,
\[
\sno u = C \, e^{\frac{i\pi u}{2K}} \, \frac{\Theta(u+iK')}{\Theta u},
\]

Now, to determine~$C$, write $u-iK'$ for~$u$; this gives
\[
k\,\sno u = C \, e^{\frac{i\pi(u-iK')}{2K}} \, \frac{\Theta u}{\Theta(u-iK')};
\]
and again changing $u$ into~$-u$,
\[
-\sno u = C \, e^{-\frac{i\pi(u+iK')}{2K}} \, \frac{\Theta u}{\Theta(u+iK')};
\]
whence, multiplying these last two equations,
\[
-k\,\sno^2 u = C^2 \cdot \frac{e^{\frac{i\pi u}{2K} - \frac{\pi K'}{2K}} \cdot e^{-\frac{i\pi u}{2K} - \frac{\pi K'}{2K}} \cdot \Theta^2 u}{\Theta(u-iK')\,\Theta(u+iK')};
\]

\newpage
or
\[
-k = C^2 \cdot e^{-\frac{\pi K'}{K}} \cdot \frac{1}{\Theta^2 0},
\]
whence
\[
\log C = \frac{1}{2}\log k - \frac{\pi K'}{2K} + \log \Theta 0,
\]
\[
k\,\sno u = \frac{\mathrm{H}(u)}{\Theta u} \qquad(3);
\]
and the equations (1), (2) and (3) may be considered as comprehending the theory of the functions $\mathrm{H}(u)$, $\Theta(u)$. The preceding process is, in fact, the converse of that made use of in the Fund.\ Nova; Jacobi having obtained for $\sno u$ an expression in the form of a fraction, takes the numerator of it for $\mathrm{H}(u)$ and the denominator for $\Theta(u)$, and thence deduces the equations (1),~(2), the intermediate steps of the demonstration being conducted by means of infinite series; the necessity of which is avoided by the preceding investigation.

I proceed to investigate certain results relating to these functions, and to the theory of elliptic functions which have been given by Jacobi in two papers, ``Suite des notices sur les fonctions elliptiques,'' Crelle, t.~III. [1828] p.~306, and t.~iv. [1829] p.~185, but without demonstration.

In the first place, the equation
\[
\frac{d^2z}{du^2} - 2\frac{E-K}{K}\,u\,\frac{dz}{du} + 2k^2k'^2\,z = 0 \qquad(4)
\]
is satisfied by $z = \Theta(u)$ or $z = \mathrm{H}(u)$. It will be sufficient to prove this for $z = \Theta(u)$, since a similar demonstration may easily be found for the other value. The following preliminary formul\ae\ will be required:
\begin{align*}
\frac{dk}{dk} &= \frac{k'^2}{k}, & \frac{dK}{dk} &= \frac{E-k'^2K}{kk'^2},\\
\frac{dE}{dk} &= \frac{E-K}{k}, &
KK' - EK' - E'K &= -\tfrac{1}{2}\pi,
\end{align*}
which are all of them known.

Now, writing $\Theta(u)$ under the slightly more convenient form
\[
\Theta u = C\,e^{\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{E-K}{K}\right)u^2} \int_0^{2K} \!\!\int_0^{2K} du\,du\,\dno^2 u - \frac{u^2}{2K}E,
\]
we have
\[
\frac{d\,\Theta u}{du} = \left(\int_0^u du\,\dno^2 u - \frac{E}{K}\,u\right)\Theta u = u\left(k'^2 - \frac{E}{K}\right) + \int_0^u du\,\cno^2 u\,\Theta u,
\]
\[
\frac{d^2\,\Theta u}{du^2} = \left\{\dno^2 u - \frac{E}{K} + \left(\int_0^u du\,\dno^2 u - \frac{E}{K}\,u\right)^2\right\}\Theta u,
\]

\newpage
The success of the process depends upon a transformation of the double integral to effect this we have
\[
\int_0^u du \int_0^u du \,\frac{d}{dk}\,\dno^2 u;
\]
\[
\frac{d}{dk}\,\dno^2 u = -\frac{2k}{k'^2}\,\sno u\,(\sno u + k^2\,\frac{d}{dk}\,\sno u);
\]
but, by a known formula,
\[
k'^2\,\frac{d}{dk}\,\sno u = -k\,\cno u\,\dno u \int_0^u \cno^2 u\,du + k\,\cno^2 u\,\sno u;
\]
hence
\[
\sno u + k\,\frac{d}{dk}\,\sno u = \frac{1}{k'^2}\,\sno u\,\dno^2 u - \frac{k}{k'^2}\,\cno u\,\dno u \int_0^u \cno^2 u\,du,
\]
whence
\begin{align*}
\frac{d}{dk}\,\dno^2 u &= -\frac{2k}{k'^4}(\sno^2 u\,\dno^2 u - k^2\,\sno u\,\cno u\,\dno u \int_0^u \cno^2 u\,du) \\
&= -\frac{2k}{k'^4}\,\sno^2 u\,\dno^2 u + \frac{2k^3}{k'^4}\,\cno^2 u \int_0^u \cno^2 u\,du;
\end{align*}
\begin{multline*}
\frac{d}{dk}\,\int_0^u du \int_0^u du\,\dno^2 u = -\frac{2k}{k'^4}\left\{\int_0^u du \int_0^u du\,\sno^2 u\,\dno^2 v + \frac{1}{2}k^2 \int_0^u du\,(\cno^2 u \int \!du \cno^2 u - \int \!du \cno^4 u)\right\} \\
= -\frac{2k}{k'^4}\left\{\int_0^u \!du \int_0^u \!du\,(2\sno^2 u\,\dno^2 u - k^2\cno^4 u) + \tfrac{1}{2}k^2 (\int_0^u \!du \cno^2 u)^2\right\}.
\end{multline*}
But
\[
\frac{d^2}{du^2}\,\sno^2 u = 2(\cno^2 u\,\dno^2 u - \sno^2 u\,\dno^2 u - k^2\,\sno^2 u\,\cno^2 u) = 2(k'^2 - 2\sno^2 u\,\dno^2 u + k^2\cno^4 u);
\]
or, integrating,
\[
\sno^2 u = k'^2 u^2 - 2\int_0^u du \int_0^u du\,(2\sno^2 u\,\dno^2 u - k^2\cno^4 u);
\]
whence at length
\[
\frac{d}{dk}\,\int_0^u du \int_0^u du\,\dno^2 u = -\frac{k}{2k'^4}\,u^2 + \frac{k}{2k'^4}\,\psi_n \sno^2 u + \frac{k^3}{2k'^4}(\int_0^u du\,\cno^2 u)^2.
\]
Also
\[
\frac{d}{dk}\,\frac{E-K}{K} = \frac{1}{k}\,\frac{E-K}{k'^2K} - \frac{1}{K}\,\frac{dE}{dk} + \frac{E}{K^2}\,\frac{dK}{dk} = \frac{1}{kk'^2} - \frac{E}{kk'^2K} - \frac{E-K}{kK} + \frac{E(E-k'^2K)}{kk'^2K^2},
\]
so that
\begin{multline*}
\frac{d^2\,\Theta u}{du^2} - 2\frac{E-K}{K}\,u\,\frac{d\,\Theta u}{du} + 2k^2k'^2\,\Theta u \\
= \left\{\frac{d^2}{du^2} - 2\frac{E-K}{K}\,u\,\frac{d}{du} + 2k^2k'^2\right\}\Theta u = 0.
\end{multline*}

\newpage
Substituting these values of $\frac{d^2\,\Theta u}{du^2}$, $\frac{d\,\Theta u}{du}$, $\Theta u$ and $\frac{d\,\Theta u}{du}$ in the equation~(4) in the place of the corresponding differential coefficients of~$z$, all the terms vanish, or the equation is satisfied by $z = \Theta(u)$, and similarly it would be satisfied by $z = \mathrm{H}(u)$.

Assume now
\[
\omega = \frac{\pi K'}{2K}, \quad v = \frac{\pi u}{2K},
\]
then observing the equation
\[
KK' - EK' - E'K = -\tfrac{1}{2}\pi,
\]
we have
\[
\frac{du}{dv} = \frac{2K}{\pi}, \quad \frac{du^2}{dv^2} = \frac{4K^2}{\pi^2}, \quad \frac{d}{du} = \frac{\pi}{2K}\,\frac{d}{dv}, \quad \frac{d^2}{du^2} = \frac{\pi^2}{4K^2}\,\frac{d^2}{dv^2},
\]
whence, substituting in the equation~(4), this becomes
\[
\frac{d^2z}{dv^2} - 4\omega\,\frac{dz}{dv} + z = 0 \qquad(5)
\]
which is of course satisfied as before by $z = \Theta(u)$, or $z = \mathrm{H}(u)$, an equation demonstrated in a different manner (by means of expansions) by Jacobi in the Memoirs referred to.

Consider next the equation
\[
\frac{d^2z}{du^2} - 2n\,\frac{E-K}{K}\,u\,\frac{dz}{du} + 2nk^2k'^2\,z = 0 \qquad(6),
\]
($n$~being any positive integer number). Then, by assuming
\[
\omega = n\,\frac{\pi K'}{2K}, \quad v = \frac{n\pi u}{2K},
\]
we should be led as before to the equation~(5). Hence, considering $\Theta(u)$ or $\mathrm{H}(u)$ as functions of $u$~and~$\frac{K'}{K}$, the equation~(6) is satisfied by assuming for~$z$ a corresponding function of $nu$ and $\frac{nK'}{K}$. Let $\lambda$~be the modulus corresponding to a transformation of the $n$\textsuperscript{th} order; then $\Lambda$,~$\Lambda'$ being the complete functions corresponding to this modulus. $\frac{\Lambda'}{\Lambda} = n\,\frac{K'}{K}$; so that the equation~(6) will be satisfied by assuming $z = \Theta_1(nu)$, or $z = \mathrm{H}_1(nu)$, where $\Theta_1$,~$\mathrm{H}_1$ correspond to the new modulus~$\lambda$.

\newpage
Assume now in the equation~(6),
\[
z = e^{nu^2\left(\frac{E-K}{K}\right)} \, (Kk')^{\frac{1}{2}n(n-1)} \, \Theta^n u \cdot t;
\]
Hence, substituting,
\begin{multline*}
\frac{d^2}{du^2}\,(\Theta^n u \cdot t) - 2nu\left(k'^2 - \frac{E}{K}\right)\frac{d}{du}\,(\Theta^n u \cdot t) \\
+ 2nk^2k'^2\,(Kk')^{\frac{1}{2}n(n-1)}\,\Theta^n u \cdot t = 0;
\end{multline*}
but
\[
\frac{d}{du}\,(\Theta^n u \cdot t) = n\,\Theta^{n-1} u \cdot \frac{d\Theta u}{du} \cdot t + \Theta^n u \cdot \frac{dt}{du},
\]
or effecting the differentiation, and eliminating $\frac{d^2\Theta u}{du^2}$ by means of the equation obtained from~(4) by writing $z = \Theta u$,
\begin{multline*}
(Kk')^{\frac{1}{2}n(n-1)} \, \frac{d^2}{du^2}\,[(Kk')^{-\frac{1}{2}n(n-1)}\,\Theta^n u \cdot z] \\
= \frac{d^2\,\Theta^n u}{du^2}\,z + 2\frac{d\,\Theta^n u}{du}\,\frac{dz}{du} + \Theta^n u\,\frac{d^2z}{du^2} \\
- 2n\left(k'^2 - \frac{E}{K}\right)u\left(\frac{d\,\Theta^n u}{du}\,z + \Theta^n u\,\frac{dz}{du}\right) + 2nk^2k'^2\,\Theta^n u \cdot z = 0.
\end{multline*}
Substituting in~(6) and reducing,
\[
\frac{d^2t}{du^2} + n(n-1)\,k^2\,\sno^2 u \cdot t = 0,
\]
whence
\[
\frac{d^2\log\Theta u}{du^2} = u\left(k'^2 - \frac{E}{K}\right) = k^2\,\int_0^u du\,\dno^2 u,
\]
\[
\frac{d^2\log\Theta u}{du^2} + 2nk^2\,\int_0^u du\,\cno^2 u - \frac{1}{2}n + 2nk k'^2\,\frac{d}{dk} = 0,
\]
\[
\frac{d^2\log\Theta u}{du^2} + n(n-1)\,k^2\,\sno^2 u \cdot z = 0.
\]
which is therefore satisfied by
\[
z = e^{nu^2\left(\frac{E-K}{K}\right)} \, (Kk')^{\frac{1}{2}n(n-1)} \, \Theta^n u \qquad(7);
\]
or
\[
z = e^{nu^2\left(\frac{E-K}{K}\right)} \, (Kk')^{\frac{1}{2}n(n-1)} \, \Theta^n u.
\]

\newpage
and each of these values is an algebraical function of $\sno u$, (viz.\ either a rational function or a rational function multiplied by $\cno u\,\dno u$). Also, in the transformation of the $n$\textsuperscript{th} order,
\[
\sqrt[n]{\sno\,nu} = \frac{\mathrm{H}_1(nu)}{\Theta_1(nu)},
\]
so that it is clear that the above values of~$z$ may be taken for the denominator and numerator respectively of $\sqrt[n]{\sno\,nu}$; i.e.\ these quantities each of them satisfy the equation~(7).

By assuming
\[
x = \sqrt[n]{\sno\,nu}, \quad z = \frac{d^2x}{du^2} = a\,x - \frac{1}{x^3},
\]
this becomes
\[
n(n-1)\,a^2x + (n-1)(ax^2 - 2a^2)\frac{dz}{dx} + (1-ax^2+a^2)\frac{d^2z}{dx^2} - 2n(a^2-4)\frac{dz}{dx} = 0 \qquad(8);
\]
which is therefore satisfied by assuming for~$z$ either the numerator or the denominator of $\sqrt[n]{\sno\,nu}$ (the transformation of the $n$\textsuperscript{th} order), which is the form in which the property is given by Jacobi.

In the case where $n$~is odd, the denominator is of the form
\[
B_{\frac{1}{2}(n-1)} x^{n-1} + B_{\frac{1}{2}(n-3)} x^{n-3} + \ldots + B_0 x,
\]
and then the numerator is
\[
x\,(B_{\frac{1}{2}(n-1)} x^{n-1} \ldots + B_1 x^{n-2} + B_0 x^{-1}),
\]
where
\[
B_0 = \frac{1}{\sqrt[n]{\sno\,K}}, \quad B_{\frac{1}{2}(n-1)} = \frac{1}{\sqrt[n]{\sno\,(K+iK')}},
\]
and all the remaining coefficients may be determined from these, the modular equation being supposed known. But the principal use of the formula is for the multiplication of elliptic functions, which it is well known corresponds to the case where $n$~is a square number. Writing $n = \nu^2$, when $\nu$~is odd, the denominator is
\[
1 + B_2 x^{\nu^2-2} \ldots + B_{\frac{1}{2}(\nu^2-3)} x^{\nu^2-1} \pm \nu x^{\nu^2-1},
\]
(the $\pm$ sign according as $\nu = (4p+1)$ or $(4p-1)$); and the numerator is obtained from this by multiplying by~$x$ and reversing the order of the coefficients. When $\nu$~is even the denominator is
\[
1 + B_2 x^{\nu^2-2} \ldots + B_{\frac{1}{2}\nu^2-1} x^{\nu^2-2} \pm x^{\nu^2},
\]
($+$ or $-$, according as $\nu = 4p$ or $\nu = 4p+2$), so that there are only half as many coefficients to be determined; but then the numerator must be separately investigated.

\newpage
In general, by leaving $n$ indeterminate, and integrating in the form of a series arranged according to ascending powers of~$x^2$; then, whenever $n$~is a square number, the series terminates and gives the denominator of the corresponding formula of multiplication; but the general form of the coefficients has not hitherto been discovered. By writing $\frac{1}{x}$ instead of~$x$, and then making $n$ infinite, the equation~(8) takes the form
\[
\frac{d^2z}{dx^2} + \frac{1-a}{x}\,\frac{dz}{dx} - 2(a^2-4)\,z = 0 \qquad(8\,\text{bis}),
\]
and it is worth while, before attempting the solution of the general case, to discuss this more simple one\footnote{Writing $(\beta+2)$ for~$a$, and putting $z = e^{\beta u^2}\,p$, this becomes
\[
\frac{d^2p}{du^2} + 2\beta u\,\frac{dp}{du} + 2\beta\,p = 0;
\]
and if $p = \Sigma Z_n$,
\[
Z_n = \frac{(-)^n}{n!}\,(\beta u^2)^n \times \frac{1}{n!}\,(\beta u^2)^n;
\]
$(8n+1)\,Z_n = \left(\frac{d}{d(\beta u^2)}\right)^n (Z_0 \cdot 2\beta u^2)$;
\[
Z_n = 2n(2n-1)\,Z_{n-1} - \beta u^2 \cdot Z_{n-2};
\]
from which the successive values of $Z_0$,~$Z_1$, \&c.\ might be calculated.}.

Assume
\[
z = 1 + C_1 x^2 + C_2 \frac{x^4}{1.2} + \ldots + C_r \frac{x^{2r}}{1.2\ldots 2r} + \ldots,
\]
then it is easy to obtain
\[
C_{r+1} = -(2r+1)(2r+2)\,C_r - (2r+2)\,a^2\,C_{r+1} + 2(a^2-4)\,C_r'.
\]
The general form may be seen to be
\[
C_r = (-)^{r+1}\{2^{2r-2}\,C_r'\,a^{2r-2} + 2^{2r-4}\,C_r''\,a^{2r-4} + \ldots\},
\]
and then
\[
C_{r+1}'' - p\,C_r'' = -r(2r-1)\,C_{r-1}'' + 16(r+2-2p)\,C_r^{(p-1)}.
\]
The complete value of $C_r^p$ (assuming $C_r^0 = 0$) is given by an equation of the form
\[
C_r^p = {}^0C_r^p + {}^1C_r^p\,2^2 + {}^2C_r^p\,2^4 \ldots + {}^{p-1}C_r^p\,2^{2p-2},
\]
where ${}^0C_r^p$, ${}^1C_r^p$ are algebraical functions of~$r$ of the degrees $2p-2$, $2p-4$, \&c.\ respectively; but as I am not able completely to effect the integration, and my only object being to give an idea of the law of the successive terms, it will be sufficient to consider the first or algebraical term ${}^0C_r^p$, which is determined by the same equation as $C_r^p$, and is moreover completely determined by this equation and the single additional

\newpage
relation $C_1^1 = 1$, since the arbitrary constants of the integration affect only the terms multiplied by $2^2$, $3^2$, \&c.

Assume
\[
C_r^p = \frac{(-)^{r+1}}{2^{2p-2}}\{2^{2p-2}\,L^p\,[r-2]^{2p-2} + 2^{2p-4}\,M^p\,[r-3]^{2p-4} + \ldots 2^{-p+1}\,X^p\,[r-2p]^2\};
\]
and substituting this value,
\begin{align*}
(1-p)\,L^p &= (1-p)\{L^{p-1} - \ldots\},\\
(1-p)\,M^p - 2p(2-2p)\,L^p &= (1-p)\{M^{p-1} - 14L^{p-1}\ldots\},\\
(1-p)\,N^p - 2p(3-2p)\,M^p &= (1-p)\{N^{p-1} - 7M^{p-1} + 122^{p-1}\},\\
(1-p)\,O^p - 2p(4-2p)\,N^p &= (1-p)\{O^{p-1} - 3N^{p-1} + 30M^{p-1}\},
\end{align*}
the law of which is obvious, the coefficients on the second side in the $q$\textsuperscript{th} line being $1$, $4q-19$, and $(2q-3)(2q-2)$ respectively. By successive integrations and substitutions
\begin{align*}
L^p - L^{p-1} &= 0, & L^p &= 1,\\
M^p - M^{p-1} &= 4p-11, & M^p &= (p-1)(2p-7),\\
N^p - N^{p-1} &= -8p^2 + 26p + 49p - 114;
\end{align*}
(the constants determined by $M^1 = 0$, $N^1 = 0$, $O^1 = 0$, $P^1 = 0$,\ldots\ so as to make $C_r^p$ contain positive powers only of~$r$).

The following are a few of the complete values of~$C_r^p$, the constants determined so as to satisfy $C_{p+1}^p = 0$ (except $C_1^1 = 1$), and the factorials being partially developed in powers of~$r$, viz.
\begin{align*}
C_1^1 &= 1,\\
C_2^2 &= (r-3)(2r-7),\\
C_3^2 &= \tfrac{1}{2}(r-4)(r-5)(4r^2 - 24r + 51),\\
C_4^2 &= \tfrac{1}{6}\{(r-5)(r-6)(r-7)(8r^3 - 60r^2 + 286r + 63) + 384(9r^2 - 93r + 242 - 2 \cdot 4^{r-2})\},\\
\&\text{c.}
\end{align*}
(it is curious that $C_2^4$, $C_3^4$, $C_4^4$ all three of them vanish). It seems hopeless to continue this investigation any further.

Returning to the equation~(8), and assuming for~$z$ an expression of the same form as before, we have, corresponding to the equations before found for the coefficients~$C_r$,
\begin{multline*}
a_{r+1} = -(2r+1)(2r+2)(n-2r)(n-2r-1)\,a_r \\
- (2r+2)(n-2r-2)\,a^2\,a_{r+1} + 2n(a^2-4)\,a_r'.
\end{multline*}

\newpage
The case corresponding to the denominator in the multiplication of elliptic functions is that of $a_0 = 1$, $a_1 = 0$. It is easy to form the table---
\begin{align*}
C_0 &= 1,\\
C_1 &= 0,\\
C_2 &= -2n(n-1),\\
C_3 &= 8n(n-1)(n-4)\,a,\\
C_4 &= -4n(n-1)(n-4)[n+7a] - 32n(n-1)(n-4)(n-9)\,a^2,\\
C_5 &= 96n(n-1)(n-4)(n-9)[n+4a]\,a \\
  &\quad+ 128n(n-1)(n-4)(n-9)(n-16)\,a^3,\\
C_6 &= -24n(n-1)(n-4)(n-9)[17n^2 + 403n + 9000] \\
  &\quad- 960n(n-1)(n-4)(n-9)(n-16)[n+41]\,a^2 \\
  &\quad- 512n(n-1)(n-4)(n-9)(n-16)(n-25)\,a^4,\\
C_7 &= +96n(n-1)(n-4)(n-9)(n-16)[79n^2 + 2825n + 36180]\,a \\
  &\quad+ 7168n(n-1)(n-4)(n-9)(n-16)(n-25)[n+42]\,a^3 \\
  &\quad+ 2048n(n-1)(n-4)(n-9)(n-16)(n-25)(n-36)\,a^5,\\
C_8 &= -48n(n-1)(n-4)(n-9)[283n^4 - 26978n^3 + 277827n^2 - 5491932n + 127764000] \\
  &\quad- 3840n(n-1)(n-4)(n-9)(n-16)(n-25)[23n^2 + 1069n + 23436]\,a^2 \\
  &\quad- 15360n(n-1)(n-4)(n-9)(n-16)(n-25)(n-36)[3n+133]\,a^4 \\
  &\quad- 8192n(n-1)(n-4)(n-9)(n-16)(n-25)(n-36)(n-49)\,a^6,\\
\&\text{c.}
\end{align*}
in which of course the coefficient of the highest power of~$n$, in the successive coefficients~$C_r$, is the value of~$C_r$ obtained from the equation~$(8\,\text{bis})$. With regard to the law of these coefficients I have found that
\begin{align*}
C_r &= (-)^{r+1}\,2^{2r-2}\,n(n-1^2)\ldots\{n-(r-1)^2\}\,b_r^0\,a^{r-1} \\
  &\quad+ 2^{2r-4}\,n(n-1^2)\ldots\{n-(r-2)^2\}\,b_r^1\,a^{r-2} \\
  &\quad+ 2^{2r-6}\,n(n-1^2)\ldots\{n-(r-3)^2\}\,b_r^2\,a^{r-3} \\
  &\quad+ \text{\&c.}
\end{align*}
(where however the next term does not contain, as would at first sight be supposed, the factor $n(n-1^2)\ldots\{n-(r-4)^2\}$). And then
\begin{align*}
C_1^0 &= 1,\\
C_2^1 &= (r-3)[n(2r-7) + (r-1)(8r-7)],\\
C_3^2 &= \tfrac{1}{2}(r-4)(r-5)[n^2(4r^2-24r+51) \\
  &\quad+ n(32r^3-220r^2+412r-255) \\
  &\quad+ 2(r-1)(r-2)(32r^2-88r+151)].
\end{align*}

\begin{center}
38---2
\end{center}

\newpage
In conclusion may be given the following results, in which, recapitulating the notation
\[
x = \sqrt[4]{k}\,\sno u, \quad a = k + \frac{1}{k}, \quad \mu = \surd(1 - ax^2 + x^4),
\]
\begin{align*}
\sqrt[4]{k}\,\sno\,2u &= \frac{2x\mu}{1-x^4},\\
\sqrt[4]{k}\,\sno\,4u &= \frac{4x\mu\,\{2 - (2a+16a^2)\,x^4 + 32ax^8 - 20x^{12} + x^{16}\}}{1 - 20x^4 + (62+16a^2)\,x^8 - 80ax^{12} + (16+64a^2)\,x^{16} - 80ax^{20} + (62+16a^2)\,x^{24} - 20x^{28} + x^{32}},\\
\sqrt[4]{k}\,\sno\,5u &= x\,(5 - 20ax^2 + (62+16a^2)\,x^4 - 80ax^6 - 10x^8 + 360ax^{10} \\
  &\quad- (300+240a^2)\,x^{12} + (368a+64a^3)\,x^{14} - (125+160a^2)\,x^{16} + 160ax^{18} \\
  &\quad- 50x^{20} + x^{24}) \\
  &\div\{1 - 50x^4 + 160ax^6 - (125+160a^2)\,x^8 + (368a+64a^3)\,x^{10} \\
  &\quad- (300+240a^2)\,x^{12} + 360ax^{14} - 10x^{16} - 80ax^{18} \\
  &\quad+ (62+16a^2)\,x^{20} - 20ax^{22} + 5x^{24}\}
\end{align*}
\&c.

Thus, writing $-x^2$ for~$x^2$, $k=1$, and therefore $a=2$,
\[
\tan 3u = \frac{x\,(3+8x^2+6x^4-x^6)}{1-6x^4-8x^6-3x^8} = \frac{x\,(3-x^2)\,(1+x^2)^2}{(1-3x^2)\,(1+x^2)^2} = \frac{x(3-x^2)}{1-3x^2},
\]
where $x = \tan u$. (And in general in reducing $\tan nu$ the extraneous factor in the numerator and denominator is $(1+x^2)^{\frac{1}{2}n(n-1)}$.)

% =====================================================================
